# GRAMMAR BANK

## 3A Simple Present + and
- I speak English.
- Americans like fast food.
- My mother cooks great food.
- I don't drink tea.
- We don't live in a large city.
- He doesn't play the guitar.

### 3.5
We use the simple present for things that are generally true or are habits.
Contractions: don't = do not, doesn't = does not.
To make negatives, we use don't / doesn't + verb (base form).
He doesn't work. Not He doesn't works.

### Spelling Rules for He / She / It
| +                              | -                               |
|--------------------------------|---------------------------------|
| I work / play / live.          | He works / plays / lives.       |
| I work.                        | I watch / finish / go / do.     |
| You work.                      | I don't work.                   |
| He / She / It works.          | You don't work                  |
| We work.                       | He / She / It doesn't work.     |
| You work.                      | We don't work.                  |
| They work.                     | You don't work.                 |
|                                | They don't work.                |

The spelling rules for the he / she / it forms are the same as for regular plurals (see Grammar Bank 2A p.126).
Be careful with some he / she / it forms:
- I have → he has /hæez/ (Not he haves)
- I go → he goes /gooz/
- I do → he does /daz/

## 3B Simple Present ?
- "Do you live in New York?"    "No, we don't."
- "Does he work at night?"      "Yes, he does."

### 3.9
We use do (or does with he, she, it) + base form to make questions.
The word order for simple present questions is auxiliary (do, does), subject (I, you, he, she, etc.), base form (work, live, etc.).

| ?                       | 5                        | X                                |
|------------------------|-------------------------|----------------------------------|
| Do I work?             | I do.                  | I don't.                        |
| Do you work?           | you do.                | you don't.                      |
| Does he / she / it work? | Yes, he / she / it does. | No, he / she / it doesn't.      |
| Do we work?            | we do.                 | we don't.                       |
| Do you work?           | you do.                | you don't.                      |
| Do they work?          | they do.               | they don't.                     |

### do and does
- do = /du/, does = /dΛz/
- do and does can be:
1. the auxiliary verb to make simple present questions.
- Do you speak English?
- Does she live here?
2. a normal verb.
- I do my homework in the evening.
- He does yoga every day.

## 3C Word Order in Questions
### Questions with Be
| Question               | be                       |
|-----------------------|-------------------------|
| Is     she from Peru? |                         |
| Are     your friends here? |                     |
| What's your dog's name? |                       |
| Where's your office?  |                         |
| How many students are in the class? |           |
| How     are you?      |                         |
| How old is she?      |                         |

### Questions with Other Verbs
| Question                 | Auxiliary  | Subject | Base form       |
|-------------------------|------------|---------|------------------|
| Do         you          | live near here? | | |
| Does       your mother   | know?     | | |
| do         you          | do?       | | |
| does       he           | live?     | | |
| How many children       | do         you          | have?     | |
| What kind of music      | does       she          | like?     | |
| How                      | do         you          | spell your last name? | |

Remember the word order in questions with be. We put be before the subject.
The word order for simple present questions with do and does is auxiliary, subject, base form, e.g., Do you live here? OR question word, auxiliary, subject, base form, e.g., Where do you live?
We often use question phrases beginning with What, e.g., What color...?, What time...?, etc.

# GRAMMAR BANK

## 4A possessive 's, Whose..?

1. He's Brad Pitt's brother. It's James's laptop.
2. It's my parents' car.
3. "Whose bag is this?" "It's Maria's."
4. The end of the movie is fantastic. I live near the city park.

### 1. We use a person + 's to talk about family and possessions.
- He's Brad Pitt's brother. Not He's the brother of Brad Pitt.

### 2. With regular plural nouns we put the ' after the s.
- It's my parents' car. Not It's my parent's car.
- With irregular plural nouns, e.g., children, men, we use 's, e.g., the children's room, men's clothes.

### 3. We use Whose...? to ask about possessions.
- We can ask Whose bag is this? OR Whose is this bag?
- We can answer It's Maria's bag. OR It's Maria's.

### 4. We don't usually use a thing + 's, e.g., the end of the class NOT the class's end, the city park NOT the city's park.

### 's
Be careful with 's. It can be two things:
- Maria's mother - 's = of Maria
- Maria's Spanish - 's = is

### Whose / Who's
- Who's = Who is, e.g., "Who's that girl?" "She's my sister."
- Whose = of who, e.g., "Whose bag is this?" "It's Jack's."
- Whose and Who's are pronounced the same /huz/.

## 4B prepositions of time and place

### Time
- in
- the morning
- the afternoon
- the evening
- the summer
- December
- 2018
- on
- Monday (morning)
- January 1
- the weekend
- at
- three o'clock
- noon / midnight
- lunchtime
- night

### Place and movement
1. He has lunch at work.
2. He works in an office.
3. He goes to work at 8:00.

### 1. We use at and in for place.
- We use at + work, home, school.
- We use in + other places, e.g., a house, an office, a room.
- We can use in or at with some public places, e.g., a restaurant, the movies, etc.
- On Saturdays, he usually has lunch in / at a restaurant.

### 2. We use to for movement or direction.
- She goes to the gym. Not She goes at the gym.
- We don't use to before home.
- go home Not go to home

## 4C position of adverbs, expressions of frequency

1. I always watch TV in the evening.
2. Do you usually sleep eight hours a day?
3. She sometimes plays sports.
4. She doesn't often go to bed late.
5. They're hardly ever late.
6. He isn't often stressed.
7. Are you usually in this classroom?

### 2. Adverbs of frequency go after be in sentences.
- In with be, the adverb of frequency goes after the subject.
- We use a + verb with hardly ever and never.
- He's never stressed. Not He isn't never stressed.

### 3. Expressions of frequency usually go at the end of a sentence or verb phrase.

# GRAMMAR BANK

## 5A can / can't
1. I can sing, but I can't dance.
2. I can come on Tuesday, but I can't come on Wednesday.
3. You can park here. You can't park there.
4. Can you help me? Can I open the window?

**can + base form has different meanings:**
1. I can (sing) = I know how to.
I can't (dance) = I don't know how to.
2. I can (come) = It's possible for me.
I can't (come) = It's not possible for me.
3. You can (park here) = It's OK. / It's permitted.
You can't (park here) = It's not OK. / It's not permitted.
4. Can you (help me)? = Please do it.
Can I (open the window)? = Is it OK if I do it?

| +                          | -                          | | |
|---------------------------|---------------------------|---|---|
| I / You / He / She / It   | can    come.              | I / You / He / She / It   | can't come. |
| We / They                 | help.                     | We / They                 | can't help. |

| ?                          | +                          | -                          | |
|---------------------------|---------------------------|---------------------------|---|
| I/you/                    | swim?                     | I/you/                    | I/you/       |
| Can he / she / it / come? | Yes, he / she / it can.   | No, he / she / it can't.  | |
| We / They                 | help?                     | We / They                 | we / they    |

**can and can't are the same for all persons (I, you, he, etc.).**
NOT He cans.

**Contraction:** can't = cannot.
We don't use to after can.
I can swim. NOT I can to swim.

## 5B present continuous
They're having a party next door.
Oh no! The baby's crying.
It's raining.

**We use the present continuous for things that are happening now.**
At the moment can mean around now.
A: What are you doing?
B: I'm reading a good book at the moment.
(= not exactly now)

**We also use the present continuous with longer periods of time, e.g., today, this week, this month.**
The present continuous emphasizes that the action is temporary, not a habit.
I'm working at home this week because my daughter isn't very well.

| +                          | -                          |
|---------------------------|---------------------------|
| I'm                       | I'm not                   |
| You're                    | You aren't                |
| He / She / It            | He / She / It isn't       |
| We're                     | We aren't                 |
| They're                   | They aren't               |

| ?                          | V                          | X                          | |
|---------------------------|---------------------------|---------------------------|---|
| Am I                      | I am.                    | I'm not.                  | |
| Are you                   | having                    | Yes, you are.             | No, you aren't.          |
| Is he / she / it         | a party?                 | he / she / it is.        | he / she / it isn't.     |
| Are we                    | we are.                  | we aren't.                | |
| Are they                  | they are.                | they aren't.              | |

## 5C simple present or present continuous?
| simple present            | present continuous        |
|---------------------------|---------------------------|
| We use the simple present to say what we usually do, or things that are normally true. | We use the present continuous to say what is happening now or around now. |
| It always snows here in the winter. | Look! It's snowing. |
| What do you usually do at work? | What are you doing now? |
| My sister works in a bank. | Today, she's working at home. |
| We often use the simple present with adverbs and expressions of frequency, e.g., always, often, once a week, etc. | We often use the present continuous with at the moment, today, this week. |

| A                         | B                         |
|---------------------------|---------------------------|
| What do you do? (= What's your job?) | I'm a teacher. |
| What are you doing? (= now, at the moment) | I'm waiting for a friend. |

# GRAMMAR BANK

## 6A Object Pronouns

| Subject Pronoun | Object Pronoun | Example                          |
|------------------|----------------|----------------------------------|
| me               | Can you help me? | |
| you              | I love you.    | |
| he               | She doesn't love him. | |
| she              | He calls her every day. | |
| it               | I don't like it. | |
| we               | Wait for us!   | |
| they             | Please help them. | |

Pronouns take the place of nouns.
We use subject pronouns when the noun is the subject of a verb (i.e., the person who does the action).
*Example:* John is a doctor. He lives in Houston.

We use object pronouns when the noun is the object of a verb (i.e., the person who receives the action).
*Example:* Anna knows John. She sees him every week.
Object pronouns go after the verb.
*Example:* I love you. Not *you love*.

### Object Pronouns After Prepositions
We also use object pronouns after prepositions (with, to, from, etc.).
*Examples:*
- I'm in love with her.
- Not I'm in love with she.
- Give this money to him.
- Not Give this money to he.

## 6B Like + (Verb + -ing)

| Base Form | Verb + -ing | Spelling Rules |
|-----------|--------------|-----------------|
| cook      | cooking      | +-ing           |
| read      | reading      | +-ing           |
| study     | studying     | one vowel + one consonant: double consonant +-ing |
| try       | trying       | one vowel + one consonant: double consonant +-ing |
| dance     | dancing      | e -ing          |
| live      | living       | e -ing          |
| shop      | shopping     | one vowel + one consonant: double consonant +-ing |
| swim      | swimming     | one vowel + one consonant: double consonant +-ing |

- I love shopping.
- I like going to the movies.
- I don't mind getting up early.
- I don't like doing housework.
- I hate driving at night.

We use verb + -ing after like, love, don't mind, and hate.
We can also use verb + -ing after enjoy and prefer.
*Example:* I enjoy watching soccer on TV, but I prefer being there.

## 6C Be or Do?

| Be                     | Do / Does                          |
|-----------------------|------------------------------------|
| Hi. I'm Jim.          | 1 I'm doing my homework.           |
| She isn't very friendly. | Do you do your homework regularly? |
| Are you Thai?         | 2 Do you speak English?            |
| I can't talk. I'm driving. | Where do they live?              |
| They aren't working today. | They don't have children.       |
| Is it raining?        | Does your sister have a car?      |
|                       | Where does your father work?       |

1. We use be as a main verb.
2. We also use be to form the present continuous. Be here is an auxiliary verb.
Remember to invert be and the subject to make questions.
*Example:* He's Spanish. Is he Spanish?

1. We use do as a main verb.
2. We also use do / does to make questions and don't / doesn't to make negatives in the simple present. Do here is an auxiliary verb.
Remember the word order for simple present questions:
auxiliary, subject, base form OR question word, auxiliary, subject, base form (see Grammar Bank 3C p.128).

# GRAMMAR BANK

## 7A Simple Past of Be: Was / Were
- Vincent van Gogh was an artist.
- Was he Dutch or French?
- I / He / She / It was there.
- I / He / She / It wasn't there.
- She wasn't in class yesterday.
- The Beatles were famous in the 1960s.
- You / We / They were there.
- You / We / They weren't there.
- Where were you last night? You weren't at home.

We use was / were to talk about the past.
We often use was / were with past time expressions, e.g., yesterday, last night, in 2014, etc.
We use was / were with born.
- I was born in Canada.

## 7B Simple Past: Regular Verbs
1. I booked the flights yesterday.
2. We arrived at the airport at 10:00 this morning.
3. When I was young, I watched TV every night.
4. I worked as a waiter every weekend when I was in college.

We use the simple past for:
1. finished actions that happened once in the past.
2. finished actions that happened more than once in the past.

| Subject          | Affirmative                | Negative                     |
|------------------|---------------------------|------------------------------|
| I / You / He / She / It | worked yesterday.         | didn't work yesterday.       |
| We / They        | were there.               | weren't there.               |

| Question                     | Answer                          |
|------------------------------|---------------------------------|
| Did he / she / it work yesterday? | Yes, he / she / it did.       |
|                              | No, he / she / it didn't.      |
| Did we / they work yesterday? | Yes, we / they did.            |
|                              | No, we / they didn't.          |

Contraction: didn't = did not.
Regular verbs in the past end in -ed, e.g., worked, lived, played.
The simple past is the same for all persons (I, you, she, etc.).
We use did / didn't + base form for simple past. Did is the past of do.

| Base Form | Past     | Spelling                          |
|-----------|----------|-----------------------------------|
| watch     | watched  | add -ed                           |
| play      | played   |                                   |
| arrive    | arrived  | add -d                            |
| study     | studied  | consonant + y: -ied               |
| stop      | stopped  | one vowel + one consonant: double |
|           |          | consonant + -ed                   |

## 7C Simple Past: Irregular Verbs
- I went to Iceland in December. I didn't go to New York.
- Did you go to a party? Who did you go with?

| Base Form | Past    | Negative         |
|-----------|---------|------------------|
| buy       | bought  | didn't buy       |
| come      | came    | didn't come      |
| feel      | felt    | didn't feel      |
| find      | found   | didn't find      |
| get       | got     | didn't get       |
| go        | went    | didn't go        |
| have      | had     | didn't have      |
| know      | knew    | didn't know      |
| put on    | put on  | didn't put on    |
| say       | said    | didn't say       |
| take      | took    | didn't take      |
| think     | thought | didn't think     |
| wear      | wore    | didn't wear      |

Some verbs are irregular in the past and change their form, e.g., go → went, have → had.
We only use the irregular past form in sentences.
- I bought a bag last night.
We use the base form after did / didn't.
- Did you go out last night? Not Did you went...?
Remember the word order in questions: auxiliary, subject, base form, e.g., Did you go out last night? or question word, auxiliary, subject, base form, e.g., Where did you go?
Look at the list of irregular verbs on p.165.

### can / could
The simple past of can is could. We add not to make negatives and reverse the subject and verb to make questions.
- We could see the whole city from the plane.
- I couldn't see him. Not I didn't can see him:
- Could you wear jeans when you were at school?

# GRAMMAR BANK

## 8A simple past: regular and irregular

### was / were and could
1. The past of be is was / were, and the past of can is could.
- Gordon was Jeremy's business partner. They were at his country house.
- They could hear a strange noise.
- She wasn't at home last night. You weren't very nice to her.
- I couldn't sleep.
- Were you sick yesterday? When was he born?
- Could you see anybody in the library?

### 2 regular verbs
- I really liked the present. She wanted to be a doctor.
- She didn't enjoy the concert. They didn't arrive until very late.
- Did you watch the game last night? When did you finish the book?

### 3 irregular verbs
- I went to Paris last summer. She slept on the sofa.
- He didn't come home last night. They didn't hear the music.
- Did you speak to your sister yesterday? Where did you have lunch?

----

## 8B there is / there are, some / any + plural nouns

| singular                          | plural                           |
|----------------------------------|----------------------------------|
| There's a garage.                | There are some pictures on the wall. |
| There isn't a swimming pool.     | There aren't any plants in the room. |
| Is there a bathroom downstairs?  | Are there any neighbors with children? |

- Yes, there is.
- Yes, there are.
- No, there isn't.
- No, there aren't.

### some and any
We often use there is / isn't with a / an, and there are / aren't with some and any. We use some and any with plural nouns. Some = not an exact number, e.g., There are some eggs in the refrigerator. We use some in positive sentences and any in negative and question sentences.

### there is / there are
We use there is / there are to say that something exists or doesn't exist, e.g., There's a bathroom upstairs. There isn't a bathroom downstairs. We use there is + a singular noun and there are + plural nouns. There is is often contracted to There's. There are is not usually contracted. When we talk about a list of things, we use there is if the first word in the list is singular or there are if the first word in the list is plural.

- In my bedroom, there's a bed, two chairs, and a desk.
- In the living room, there are two armchairs and a sofa.

----

## 8C there was / there were

| singular                       | plural                   |
|-------------------------------|-------------------------|
| There was a big mirror.      | There were four lamps.  |
| There wasn't a TV.           | There weren't any ghosts.|
| Was there a bathroom?        | Were there any windows? |

- Yes, there was.
- Yes, there were.
- No, there wasn't.
- No, there weren't.

# GRAMMAR BANK

## 9A countable / uncountable nouns, a / an, some / any

| Countable                | Uncountable          | a / an, some / any          |
|-------------------------|----------------------|------------------------------|
| an apple                | rice                 | We need an apple.           |
| three apples            | meat                 | some butter.                |
|                         |                      | some apples.                |
|                         |                      | We don't need a tomato.     |
|                         |                      | any rice.                   |
|                         |                      | any tomatoes.                |
|                         |                      | ? Do we need an orange?     |
|                         |                      | any sugar?                  |
|                         |                      | any oranges?                |

English nouns can be countable or uncountable.

- **Countable** = things you can count, e.g., apples. Countable nouns can be singular (an apple) or plural (apples).
- **Uncountable** = things you can't count, e.g., rice, meat. NOT two rices, three meats. Uncountable nouns are usually singular.

Some nouns can be countable and uncountable, e.g., ice cream.

- an ice cream (countable)
- some ice cream (uncountable)

We use **a / an** with singular countable nouns. a / an = one.
We use **some** with plural countable nouns and with uncountable nouns.
We use **any** with plural countable nouns and with uncountable nouns.
We use **some** to ask for and offer things.

- Can I have some sugar, please?
- Would you like some coffee?

## 9B quantifiers

| uncountable (singular) | short answers         | full answers                |
|------------------------|-----------------------|-----------------------------|
| How much sugar do you eat? | A lot.              | I eat a lot of sugar.      |
|                        | A little.             | I eat a little sugar.      |
|                        | Not much.             | I don't eat much sugar.    |
|                        | None.                 | I don't eat any sugar.     |

| countable (plural)     | short answers         | full answers                |
|------------------------|-----------------------|-----------------------------|
| How many cookies do you eat? | A lot.           | I eat a lot of cookies.    |
|                        | A few.                | I eat a few cookies.       |
|                        | Not many.             | I don't eat many cookies.  |
|                        | None.                 | I don't eat any cookies.   |

**a lot of** and **much / many**
In affirmative sentences, we usually use a lot of.
In negative sentences and questions, we usually use much and many.

- I don't drink much water.
- Do you drink much coffee?

It is also possible to use a lot of in negative sentences and questions.

- I don't eat a lot of vegetables.
- Do you drink a lot of coffee?

**a lot of** and **lots of**
A lot of and lots of mean the same thing, e.g., He eats a lot of cheese / lots of cheese.

## 9C comparative adjectives

| adjective   | comparative   | spelling                                   |
|-------------|---------------|-------------------------------------------|
| old         | older         | one-syllable adjectives: + -er (or -r if the adjective ends in e) |
| nice        | nicer         | adjectives ending one vowel + one consonant: double consonant + -er |
| big         | bigger        | one- or two-syllable adjectives ending consonant +y: -ier |
| hot         | hotter        | one-syllable adjectives ending -ed: more + adjective |
| dry         | drier         | two- or more syllable adjectives: more + adjective |
| healthy     | healthier     |                                           |
| tired       | more tired    |                                           |
| famous      | more famous   |                                           |
| expensive   | more expensive|                                           |
| good        | better        | irregular                                 |
| bad         | worse         |                                           |
| far         | farther / further |                                       |

We use comparative adjectives + than to compare two things, people, etc.

# 10A Superlative Adjectives

It's the oldest bridge in the world.
It's the most popular shopping mall in the US.
She's the best student in the class.
Monday is the worst day of the week.

We use **the + superlative adjective** to say which is the (biggest, etc.) in a group. After superlatives, we use **in** (not **of**) + places, e.g., the longest road in the world, the tallest building in New York.

| Adjective | Superlative |
|-----------|-------------|
| cold      | the coldest |
| high      | the highest |
| big       | the biggest  |
| hot       | the hottest  |
| dry       | the driest   |
| sunny     | the sunniest  |
| bored     | the most bored |
| stressed   | the most stressed |
| dangerous | the most dangerous |
| good      | the best    |
| bad       | the worst   |
| far       | the farthest / furthest |

### Grammar Bank

| Comparative | Superlative | Spelling |
|-------------|-------------|----------|
| colder      | the coldest | one-syllable adjectives: + -est |
| higher      | the highest | adjectives ending one vowel + one consonant: double consonant + -est |
| bigger      | the biggest | one- or two-syllable adjectives ending consonant + y: + -iest |
| hotter      | the hottest | one-syllable adjectives ending -ed: the most + adjective |
| drier       | the driest  | two- or more syllable adjectives: the most + adjective |
| sunnier     | the sunniest | irregular |
| more bored  | the most bored | |
| more stressed | the most stressed | |
| more        | the most    | |
| dangerous   | dangerous    | |
| better      | the best    | |
| worse       | the worst   | |
| farther/further | the farthest / furthest | |

# 10B Be Going To (Plans), Future Time Expressions

I'm going to take a vacation next month.
I'm not going to study English.
Are you going to fly to Paris?

We use **be going to + verb (base form)** to talk about future plans. We often use future time expressions with going to, e.g., tomorrow, next week, next month, next year, etc.

| Full Form | Contraction |
|-----------|-------------|
| I am      | I'm        |
| You are   | You're     |
| He / She / It is | He / She / It's |
| We are    | We're      |
| They are  | They're    |

| Full Form | Contraction |
|-----------|-------------|
| I am not  | I'm not    |
| You are not | You aren't |
| He / She / It is not | He / She / It isn't |
| We are not | We aren't  |
| They are not | They aren't |

| ?          | V                          | X                       | |
|------------|----------------------------|-------------------------|---|
| Am I      | take a vacation            | I am.                  | I'm not.               |
| Are you    | going to next summer?     | Yes, you are.          | No, you aren't.       |
| Is he / she / it | study English        | he / she / it is.     | he / she / it isn't.  |
| Are we     | tonight?                  | we are.               | we aren't.            |
| Are they   |                            | they are.             | they aren't.          |

----

# 10C Be Going To (Predictions)

We can use **be going to + verb (base form)** to make predictions (= to say what you think or can see is going to happen in the future).

I think it's going to rain.
You're going to be very happy.
I'm sure they're going to win.

# GRAMMAR BANK

## 11A Adverbs (Manner and Modifiers)

### Adverbs of Manner

| Adjective | Adverb   | Spelling                | Very, Really, etc.          |
|-----------|----------|-------------------------|------------------------------|
| slow      | slowly   |                         | It isn't very expensive.     |
| quick     | quickly  | +-1y                    | She drives incredibly fast.  |
| bad       | badly    |                         | They speak really slowly.    |
| careful   | carefully|                         |                              |
| healthy   | healthily| consonant + y: + -ily   |                              |
| easy      | easily   |                         |                              |
| possible  | possibly | le → -ly                |                              |
| good      | well     | irregular               |                              |
| fast      | fast     |                         |                              |
| hard      | hard     |                         |                              |

We use adverbs of manner to say how people do things. Adverbs usually go after the verb or verb phrase.

- I speak English well. NOT I speak well English.

1. We usually form adverbs by adding -ly to adjectives.
2. Some adverbs are irregular. They can be the same as the adjective, e.g., fast, hard or a different word, e.g., well.

Remember the difference between adjectives and adverbs.

- I'm a careful driver. (careful is an adjective. It describes the noun, driver.)
- I drive carefully. (carefully is an adverb. It describes the verb, drive.)

Be careful. Some words that end in -ly aren't adverbs, e.g., friendly (= adjective).

- He's a friendly person.

## 11B Verb + Infinitive

1. I want to travel for six months.
2. She decided to go to Australia.
3. You need to practice every day.
4. When did you learn to play the guitar?
5. Would you like to go to Africa?
6. I wouldn't like to be famous.

Many verbs are often followed by another verb in the infinitive. These include want, need, learn, promise, decide, plan, choose, try, remember, forget, and hope.

- I would like to = I want to (now or in the future). Would like is also followed by the infinitive.

Contractions: 'd = would. wouldn't = would not.

### Would Like and Like

- I'd like to dance. = I want to dance
- I like dancing. = I enjoy it; I like it in general.

We can also use "Would you like..?" to offer, e.g., Would you like a drink? Would like is the same for all persons.

## 11C Definite Article

### The

1. We use the:
- when it is clear what we are talking about, e.g., Close the window. = the window that is open.
- when there is only one of something, e.g., the internet, the sun, etc.
- before superlative adjectives, e.g., the biggest, the best, etc.

2. We don't usually use the:
- when we talk about people or things in general.
- Men are more interested in sports than women. (general)
- BUT The women in this class work harder than the men. (specific)

### A / An or The?

We often use a the first time we mention a person or thing. The next time we use the because it is now clear what we are talking about, e.g., Let's have a pizza. The pizzas are very good here.

- before possessive 's.
- She's my mother's cousin. Not She's the my mother's cousin.

- with:
- meals: have breakfast, lunch, dinner, etc.
- by + transportation: go by car, travel by train, etc.
- general places: work, school, college, bed, home.

# GRAMMAR BANK

## 12A Present Perfect

1. A: Have you seen the new Matt Damon movie?
B: Yes, I have.
She hasn't read any books in English.

2. Have you ever read a Russian novel?
I've never worked in an office.

3. Have you finished the exercise?
Your parents have arrived. They're in the living room.

### Usage of Present Perfect
1. We use the present perfect when we talk or ask about events in the past, but when we don't say or ask when.
2. We often use the present perfect with **ever** (= at any time in your life) and **never** (= at no time in your life).
3. We also use the present perfect to talk about recent events, e.g., I've finished my homework.

### Structure
To make the present perfect, we use **have/has + the past participle** of the verb.

| Full form of have | Contraction | Past Participle | Base Form | Simple Past | Past Participle |
|-------------------|-------------|------------------|-----------|-------------|------------------|
| I have            | I've        | like             | liked     | liked       |                  |
| You have          | You've      | want             | wanted    | wanted      |                  |
| He / She / It has | He's        | seen that movie. |           |             |                  |
| We have           | We've       |                  |           |             |                  |
| They have         | They've     |                  |           |             |                  |

Past participles of regular verbs are the same as the simple past.

| Base Form | Simple Past | Past Participle |
|-----------|-------------|------------------|
| read /rid/| read/red/   | read/red/        |
| see       | saw         | seen             |

Past participles of irregular verbs are sometimes the same as the simple past, e.g., read /red/ but sometimes different, e.g., seen. Look at the list of irregular past participles on p.165.

## 12B Present Perfect or Simple Past?

A: Have you been to that new Italian restaurant?
B: Yes, I have.

A: When did you go there?
B: I went last weekend.

A: Who did you go with?
B: I went with some people from work.

We often use the present perfect to ask/tell somebody about a past action for the first time. We don't ask/say when the action happened.
**Example:** Have you been to that new Italian restaurant?
Then we use the simple past to ask/talk about the details.
**Example:** "When did you go there?" "I went last weekend."

We use the simple past **not** the present perfect with when and past time expressions, e.g., yesterday, last week.
**Example:** When did you see the movie? Not When have you seen the movie?
**Response:** I saw it last week. Not I've seen it last week.

### Comparison of "Been" and "Gone"
- **Been** is the past participle of **be**, and **gone** is the past participle of **go**.
- In the present perfect, we use **been to** (not **gone to** or **been in**) to say that somebody has visited a place.
**Example:** I've been to Tokyo three times.
- We use **gone to** when somebody goes to a place and is still there.
**Example:** My parents have gone to the US for their vacation. They're having a great time.

### Compare:
- Nick has been to Paris. = He visited Paris and came back at some time in the past.
- Nick has gone to Paris. = He went to Paris and he is in Paris now.

### b Put the verbs in parentheses in the present perfect or simple past.
A: Have you ever been to Thailand? (be)
B: No, but I **went** to South Korea last year. (go)
A: 1. **Have** you ever **visited** any countries in South America? (visit)
B: Yes, I have. I 2. **went** to Brazil a few years ago. (go)
A: 3. Who **did** you **go** with? (go)
B: My boyfriend. It was a work trip and his company 4. **paid** for everything. (pay)
A: How wonderful! 5. **Were** you there for long? (be)
B: No, we 6. **were** only there for five days. (be)

A: 7. Where **did** you **stay**? (stay)
B: We 8. **had** a suite in a five-star hotel. It was beautiful! (have)
A: 9. **Has** the company **invited** you on any other trips recently? (invite)
B: No. My boyfriend 10. **stopped** working there a year later, so that was our only trip. (stop)

**Writing and saying the date**
We write | We say
---|---
March 22nd | March twenty-second or the twenty-second of March
1/12 | January twelfth or the twelfth of January

**Prepositions with years, months, and dates**
Use in + years, e.g., The Los Angeles Olympics are in 2028.
Use in + months, e.g., My birthday's in February.
Use on + dates, e.g., The meeting is on Friday, September 5th.

**Saying years**
1807 eighteen "oh" seven
1936 nineteen thirty-six
2008 two thousand eight (for years 2000-2010)
2011 two thousand eleven OR twenty eleven

spring /sprin/
summer /'samar/
fall /fol/
winter /wintr/

**AcTivATioN** Take turns saying five things you did yesterday and five things you did last week with went, had, or got.
Yesterday, I got up early. I had breakfast in a café. I went shopping.

# Prepositions

## VOCABULARY BANK

### 1 PLACE

a Match the words and pictures.

- in /ɪn/ (the wardrobe)
- in front of /ɪn frʌnt əv/ (the table)
- on /ɒn/ (the chair)
- under /ˈʌndər/ (the bed)
- behind /bɪˈhaɪnd/ (the sofa)
- between /bɪˈtwiːn/ (the windows)
- across from /əˈkrɔs frəm/ (the woman)
- next to /nɛkst tu/ (the armchair)
- over /ˈoʊvər/ (the mirror)

b 8.22 Listen and check.

**ACTIVATION** In pairs, point and ask and answer about the pictures.
Where's the ghost? (He's under the bed.)

**above and below**
above /əˈbʌv/ is similar to over.
below /bɪˈloʊ/ is similar to under.

### 2 MOVEMENT

a Match the words and pictures.

- from /frʌm/ (the bedroom)
- to /tuː/ (the bathroom)
- into /ˈɪntuː/ (the wardrobe)
- out of /aʊt əv/ (the wardrobe)
- through /θruː/ (the window)
- up /ʌp/ (the stairs)
- down /daʊn/ (the stairs)

b 8.23 Listen and check.

**ACTIVATION** In pairs, point and ask and answer about the pictures.
Where's the ghost going? (He's going up the stairs.)
